世界経済：AI関連需要の広がりが世界景気をけん引。企業の景況感は堅調に推移
 製造業は節目50を上回る良好な水準を維持。非製造業は金融・消費関連を中心に加速
― 製造業はAI関連投資がけん引し、防衛支出の拡大も寄与。一方、中東情勢を受けた在庫積み増しの寄与は縮小傾向
― 非製造業は金融サービスやAI関連需要がけん引。エネルギー高で落ち込んでいた観光・娯楽も回復
 国・地域別では、米国では非製造業が大幅に改善。日本やユーロ圏では製造業を中心に改善

― 日本ではAI・半導体需要と外需拡大がけん引し、ユーロ圏は電気・電子部品を含む中間財の生産が好調。AI投資ブー
ムの広がりがうかがわれる
1
製造業の景況感（購買担当者景気指数） 非製造業の景況感（購買担当者景気指数）
（注） 日米欧：S&P Global、中：国家統計局。購買担当者景気指数：企業に対するアン
ケート調査結果から作成した指数。新規受注や生産高、雇用、出荷遅延などの指数
に一定のウエイトを掛けて算出。50が景気拡大・縮小の分岐点
（出所）S&P Global、中国国家統計局より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成
（注） 日米欧：S&P Global、中：国家統計局。購買担当者景気指数（非製造業）：企業に対
するアンケート調査結果から作成した指数。50が景気拡大・縮小の分岐点
（出所）S&P Global、 中国国家統計局より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成

### Page 3

金融市場概況：原油高を背景に日米金利が上昇
 日米株価は、9月下旬にハイテク株を中心に上昇
― 9月上旬は、日米株価とも軟調。原油高を背景とした日米金利上昇が重荷に
― 下旬に入ると、米AI関連株が買い戻されたことからナスダックが上昇に転じ、日経平均もつれ高の展開に
 ドル円は一時円高が進展したものの、日米金利差が拡大し、再度150円台後半まで円安が進む「往って来い」の展開に
― 日本・米国とも利上げを実施。先行きも利上げ継続見込みだが、米金利上昇幅がより大きく、日米金利差は拡大
― 日銀は物価上振れリスクに対応し、利上げペースを加速へ。次回利上げは26年12月会合と予想
2
（出所）LSEGより、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成

米国：個人消費は一時的に減速も、サービス中心に底堅さ維持
 7月実質個人消費支出は前月比＋0.0％と減速も、一時要因による下押しが大きく、消費の基調は崩れていないと評価
― 例年より前倒しで6月に実施された大手通販セールの反動から、財消費が減少
― 一方、そうした影響を受けづらいサービス消費については強さを維持。高齢化を背景に医療支出が拡大したほか、W杯
開催等を受け、娯楽サービス、外食・宿泊、輸送サービスなどの選択的支出も増加
 小売販売の高頻度データは、足元で伸びが再び高まっており、7月の減速が一時的であったことを示唆

3
（出所） 米商務省より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成

米国：所得減速下でも株高による資産効果が消費を下支え
 消費が底堅い背景に、富裕層を中心とした株価上昇による資産効果の持続
― 株高を受けてインフレ下でも家計の実質金融資産は着実に増加。可処分所得対比でも高水準を維持。実質所得が弱含
む中でも、金融資産の増加が家計の購買力を下支え
 先行きは物価鈍化に伴う実質所得の改善も消費の追い風に
― 実質可処分所得は前年比＋0.5％と伸び悩みが継続。先行きは物価鈍化を受けて実質所得も伸び幅を徐々に持ち直す
見込み
4
（出所） 米商務省より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成

### Page 6

米国：雇用は予想外の大幅増。一時的要因の影響を受けつつも労働市場の底堅さを確認
 8月の非農業部門雇用者数は前月差＋16.2万人と市場予想（同＋5.6万人）を大幅に上回る結果
― 過去2カ月も合わせて5.5万人の上方修正。速報段階で減少した7月もプラス転化
― 娯楽・宿泊（同＋6.2万人）や教育・医療（同＋2.8万人）を中心に民間就業者が伸びを高めたほか、政府部門も同＋3.5
万人増と押し上げに寄与
― 政府部門では、バックトゥスクールに伴い教員、スクールバス運転士、カフェテリアスタッフなどの雇用が増加（例年みら
れる季節的な動きであるが、統計上の調整にゆがみが生じている可能性）
5
（出所）米労働省より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成

（年初来変動幅、万人）
（年/月）
失業率は低位で横ばい
低失業率の一因に「少ない供給」
米国：失業率は低位安定。厳格化する移民政策が労働供給を一段と下押し
 失業率は4.1％と前月からほぼ横ばい。引き続きFOMC参加者が見込む長期均衡水準（Longer run）を下回る低水準
 低失業率の一因に労働参加率の低下。シニア層の退職増加に加え、移民政策の厳格化の影響も大
― 米移民・関税執行局（ICE）の摘発増など移民取り締まりが強化されるなか、メキシコ出身者などの労働人口は減少傾向
― 足元ではハイチなど複数国の一時保護資格（TPS、※）が相次ぎ終了。今後もエルサルバドルなどで有効期限を迎え、7

月以降の累計で最大100万人程度がTPSに基づく就労資格を失う可能性。労働供給を更に押し下げる要因に
（※）紛争や災害等により安全な帰国が困難な特定国の出身者に対し、一時的な在留・就労を認める制度
6
（出所）米労働省より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成
失業率 移民の出身国別労働人口とTPS終了の対象国・規模
（注）TPS保有者数は各国について入手可能な最新値。ベネズエラは2023年指定分。9月以降のTPS終了
時期は現時点での有効期限であり、今後の延長・終了決定等により変更される可能性がある
（出所）米労働省、米移民評議会、米国土安全保障省、米議会調査局より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成

米国：エネルギー高・関税でディスインフレは足踏み、期待インフレも高止まり
 エネルギー高や関税が重石となり、インフレ率の低下は足踏み
―8月のCPIが前年比＋3.4％と横ばいにとどまるなか、コアCPIも同＋2.4％（7月同＋2.5％）と減速は緩やか。サービス価
格は原油高を背景に航空運賃などが上昇、財価格には関税の影響が残存
 消費者の期待インフレ率は3％台で高止まり、2％目標への収束はなお見通しづらい状況
―FRB高官からは、5年以上に及ぶ目標超過で高インフレが定着するリスクを警戒する声も
7

や原油価格上昇の影響が大きいと考えられる品目を分類

欧州：米国と比べて鈍い消費回復ペース。実質金融資産の目減りが消費回復の重石に
8
 原油価格高騰一服を受けて、家計のマインドは底打ち
―7～9月期以降は下押し圧力が緩和する公算も、米国に比べて消費回復ペースの鈍さは否めず
 最悪期は脱しつつある一方で、実質賃金の伸び悩みは継続。高インフレによる金融資産の目減りも逆風に
―4～6月期のユーロ圏妥結賃金は前年比＋2.4％と前期（同＋2.6％）から鈍化。＋3％前後のインフレに割り負けている
状況変わらず。実質金融資産の目減りも相まって、家計の貯蓄率は高止まり
（出所）Eurostat 、米商務省より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成

欧州：ドイツでは防衛・インフラ投資の効果が一部発現。恩恵は年後半以降一段と顕在化
9
 財政拡張を支えに、ドイツでは防衛・インフラ関連業種の生産・受注が拡大
― 生産水準が急速に高まる武器・弾薬の他、足元では鉄道車両の生産も拡大
― 公共部門によるインフラ投資の受注残は直近2四半期で＋10％超の増加
 今後の財政支出余地を踏まえれば、当該業種への恩恵は年後半以降、一段と顕在化する見込み
―6月末時点の執行額は既に2025年通年の約8割に達するも、年間予算に対する執行率は4割弱にとどまる

4～6月期
７月
（前期比、％）
欧州：4～6月期の輸出拡大はエネルギー市況や特需が主因。輸出競争力の低迷は変わらず
10
 エネルギー市況や石化製品の代替調達需要増等を受け、4～6月期は輸出が拡大
―4～6月期の輸出金額は前期比＋5.6％（輸出数量：同＋2.1%）の増加。単価上昇によりエネルギーが急増した他、アジ
ア系メーカーの生産調整を受けた代替調達需要の高まり等を背景に、化学製品が増加
 ただし、底流にある輸出競争力の低迷は変わらず。AI関連財の外需寄与はネットでマイナス
―AI関連財の輸入額は年初来＋27.1％増加し、輸出の伸び（同＋14.0％）を大きく上回る
EU：AI関連財の輸出入

日本：耐久財特需一服後も、所得改善を背景に緩やかな回復基調を維持
16
 耐久財特需の押し上げは一服
― 自動車・エアコン購入の特需は徐々に剥落。食品などの非耐久財は根強い節約志向を背景に弱含み
― 一方、サービス消費は増加基調を維持。個人消費は一時的な上振れから元の緩やかな拡大トレンドへ回帰する動き
 個人消費は良好な所得環境を背景に今後も緩やかな回復基調で推移する見通し
― 実質賃金の伸びは低下も、夏季賞与要因の剥落が原因。実質所定内給与は足元でも1％程度の伸びを維持
― 懸念材料は再び上昇し始めた原油価格。高インフレが再燃すれば、個人消費の回復に水を差す懸念

日本：日銀追加利上げは最短インターバル。今後も日銀は3カ月に1回程度のペースで利上げへ
17
 日銀は9月会合で利上げを決定（反対2名）。利上げは6月以来3カ月ぶりで、今次局面では最も短いインターバル
―9月会合後の会見で植田総裁は、物価押し上げから物価上振れ抑制へと「政策の局面が変化した」と説明
― 政策金利は1.25％と、日銀による中立金利推計値のレンジ下限を上回るが、金融環境は利上げ後も緩和的と評価
 今後も日銀は3カ月に1回程度のペースで利上げを続ける可能性大
― 大幅利上げや連続利上げについては物価上振れリスクが大きい場合の選択肢と説明。一方、急激な政策変更を回避す

る観点から、先手を打って利上げを実施していく姿勢
物価上振れリスクへの対応から
利上げ間隔が短期化
政策金利とブレークイーブン・インフレ
（注） ブレークイーブン・インフレは基調的物価上昇率の一面を捉えていると考えられるが、
必ずしも基調的物価上昇率そのものを示す指標ではない点に留意
（出所）日本銀行、LSEGより、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成 （出所） 日本銀行より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成
日銀9月会合のポイント（声明文・総裁会見）
声明文
• 企業の賃金・価格設定行動の積極化や中長期的な予想物価上昇
率の上昇などを踏まえると、2％物価目標を上振れるリスクがある
• 企業間取引における価格上昇圧力が消費者段階にも波及し始め
ているほか、賃金上昇の販売価格への転嫁の動きも続いている
• 政策金利の変更後も、緩和的な金融環境は維持される
会見での注目発言
• 基調的物価上昇率が2％に接近し、政策局面が変化。物価上振れ
を回避する観点が重要に
• 原油価格の上昇やそこから波及した物価上昇圧力、AI関連需要

や円安進行が幅広い品目の物価上昇圧力に繋がる可能性
• 基調的物価の2％定着を確認するのにはしばらく時間がかかる
• 金融緩和の度合いは利上げにより少しずつ縮まっているが、銀行
貸し出しや資産市場は依然として緩和的
• 急激な利上げで金融環境が大きく変化することや、それによる経
済へのマイナス影響を回避する観点は重要
• 大幅利上げに追い込まれる可能性を減らすように政策を運営する

米国債券市場：インフレ懸念と追加利上げ観測を背景に利回りは上昇
 9月の米10年国債利回りは、インフレ懸念の高まりから5.2％まで上昇
― 月前半は、雇用統計の上振れや原油高、物価指標の高止まりから、長期金利は5％の大台に
― 中旬は、FOMCが25bpの利上げを決定。追加利上げの可能性も示唆され、長期金利は5％前後で高止まり
― 月後半は、堅調な景況感やFRB高官のタカ派発言を背景に、長期金利は5.2％台まで上昇
 今後は、ホルムズ海峡を巡る中東情勢の動向が鍵に。原油高が続けば、複数回の追加利上げにも現実味
― トランプ大統領がイランの提示した停戦案を拒否するなど、中東情勢の先行きは依然として不透明

国内債券・為替：長期金利は一時3.1％超え。ドル円は往って来いで円安継続へ
 長期金利は上昇。一時3.115％と1996年8月ぶりの水準に
― 日銀9月会合前後で利上げ前倒しを織り込む動き。米FRBの追加利上げ観測も、円安進行を通じて金利上昇の要因に
― 先行きも原油価格や米金利の高止まりが見込まれるもと、日本の長期金利も3％超での推移が続く可能性
 9月のドル円は往って来い。先行きも、150円台を中心とした円安水準が続く見込み
― 9月前半は、ベッセント財務長官の円安警戒発言や日銀利上げ観測の高まりを受け、一時152円台後半まで円高に
― もっとも、円高基調は定着せず。9月後半にかけては米利上げ観測の高まりを背景に150円台後半まで再度円安が進行

内外株式：原油高が重石も、AI関連需要を支えに日米株はともに底堅く推移
 日米株は、中東情勢の緊迫化に伴う原油高が重石となるも、AI・半導体関連需要を支えに底堅く推移
― 日経平均は月半ばの調整後、半導体関連株がけん引し上昇。S&P500も最高値圏を維持
―WTI 原油価格は、サウジアラビアの主要パイプライン停止等から一時100ドル超へ急伸。パイプラインの部分的な稼働
再開を受け月半ば以降は反落も、インフレ懸念に伴う金利上昇が株価の重石に
 日経VIは緩やかに低下、米VIXも低位横ばい圏。他方で原油・金利動向を巡る振れやすさには引き続き留意

― 中東情勢を受けた変動は一時的に留まった一方、米・イラン間の協議停滞など原油高が株価を圧迫する構図は残存

## Document 078: 261005_gs_TSMC

### Page 1

Key Data  _____________________________________

GS Forecast  __________________________________

GS Factor Profile  ______________________________

TSMC will host its 3Q26 analyst meeting on 15 October. We expect a 
strong finish to 2026 with a sustained growth outlook into 2027 for 
TSMC as demand broadens across AI GPUs/AI ASICs, networking 
and server CPUs. In particular, the stronger CPU demand driven by 
agentic AI represents a key change over the past year. We forecast 
2026E/2027E revenue growth of 42.0%/36.9% YoY (USD terms), 
with GM at 67.2%/67.5%. We also raise our 2027E/2028E CapEx 
forecasts to US$85bn/US$98bn (vs. US$78bn/US$82bn 
previously) as we expect TSMC to further ramp up its capacity 
investment to support longer-term customer demand. 
Strong sequential growth into 4Q26 with modest N2 ramp dilution 
We expect TSMC’s 3Q26E revenue to grow 15.3% QoQ, followed 
by a further 11.0% QoQ increase in 4Q26E (both in USD terms), 
supported by continued strength in AI/HPC demand. On 
profitability, we model GM edging down to 67.5%/67.3% in 
3Q26E/4Q26E, vs. 67.7% in 2Q26, reflecting dilution from the 
ongoing N2 ramp.  
Momentum sustaining into 2027, supported by broader AI demand 
We expect TSMC’s revenue to grow 36.9% YoY (vs. 32.0% previously) 
in USD terms in 2027E, supported by sustained demand for AI 
accelerators and networking, alongside an increasingly strong 
contribution from server CPUs. At our Communacopia + Technology 
conference, management highlighted that lower token costs should 
stimulate greater AI consumption, while discussions with customers 
and US CSPs has reinforced confidence in demand and 
infrastructure readiness. We believe these trends support continued 
strength in leading-edge demand, particularly for both N2 and N3, 
where management expects supply to remain insufficient despite 
further expansion.

### Page 3

On the supply side, we expect capacity additions, N5-to-N3 conversion, and 
productivity gains to support higher wafer output, while continued CoWoS expansion 
helps enable associated wafer shipments. We model GM improving modestly to 67.5% in 
2027E (vs. 67.2% in 2026E), supported by higher pricing, favorable product mix, 
sustained high utilization and continued productivity gains. 
Higher CapEx to support longer-term expansion 
We maintain our 2026E CapEx forecast at US$64bn, while raising our 2027E/2028E 
forecasts to US$85bn/US$98bn (vs. US$78bn/US$82bn previously), to incorporate 1) 
cost inflation from equipment vendors, and 2) initial spending associated with the 
potential new Texas expansion; however, we expect the new site to only enter mass 
production beyond 2032. For capacity, we continue to model our end-2027E/2028E 
N3/N2 capacity at 200kwpm/140kwpm and 220kwpm/200kwpm, respectively. 
Other key areas in focus for the upcoming analyst meeting 
Long-term AI growth outlook: We believe the broadening of AI demand across 
accelerators, CPUs and networking further supports TSMC’s long-term growth 
opportunity. We would look for updated comments from management on the key 
assumptions underpinning its AI outlook through 2030, where demand drivers have 
strengthened most over the past year, and the factors that could materially change this 
outlook. 
Longer-term CapEx outlook and US expansion: With customer demand continuing to 
drive investment in leading-edge capacity and advanced packaging, we would look for 
management to provide further direction on its longer-term CapEx trajectory and the 
demand visibility supporting these investment decisions. We would also welcome 
updated comments on whether customer demand could lead to further expansion in the 
US beyond current Arizona site, and the potential timing and scale of such investments. 
Foundry competition and technology leadership: We would look for management’s 
perspective on the evolving competitive landscape, including Intel, Samsung and 
Terafab, and the implications for TSMC’s longer-term positioning. In particular, we would 
welcome further discussion of its technology leadership, and whether management 
expects this gap to widen or narrow across upcoming process generations.

### Page 5

Earnings estimate revisions, valuation and risks

Forecast changes 
We have revised up 2026/27/28E earnings by 1%/7%/8% to reflect 1) higher GM 
assumptions as we see a milder GM dilution from N2 ramp, and 2) higher UTRs especially 
for N2/N3 given stronger demand from AI/HPC.

Reiterate Buy; TP raise to NT$3,300 from NT$3,100 
We raise our 12m TP to NT$3,300 from NT$3,100 (2330.TW), following our upward 
earnings revisions. Our TP is based on a target P/E of 22x (unchanged, benchmarked to 
1.0stdv above 5-year avg. forward P/E) applied to our higher 2027E EPS. Accordingly, we

### Page 7

Investment Thesis - TSMC (2330.TW/TSM) 
TSMC is a leading global foundry company specializing in cutting-edge nodes. Its leading 
technology stance enables it to enjoy more than 60% revenue share in the global 
foundry market. We like TSMC as we believe its solid technology leadership and 
execution better position it vs. peers to capture the industry’s long-term structural 
growth opportunities, particularly in areas such as AI/5G/HPC/EV. In addition, valuation 
looks attractive in our view, with the shares trading at the mid-range of their 10-year 
trading history (P/E: 10-29x). Furthermore, we view TSMC as the key AI enabler among 
our Taiwan Semis coverage, thanks to its leadership stance in leading edge nodes and 
advanced packaging technology – CoWoS (chip on wafer on substrate). We believe TSMC 
will achieve its 25% revenue CAGR target for the next several years, driven primarily by 
increasing silicon content growth and AI/HPC demand, with LT GM to remain at 56%+. 
We are Buy rated.

Price Target Risks and Methodology - TSMC (2330.TW/TSM) 
Valuation methodology: We have a 12m TP of NT$3,300, which is derived by applying a 
target P/E multiple of 22x to our 2027E EPS. Our 22x target P/E is benchmarked against 
1.0stdv above its 5-year trading average. For the ADR (TSM), we have a 12m TP of 
US$660, based on a USD/TWD rate of 30.0 and an ADR premium of 20%. 
Key downside risks to our views: (1) further deterioration in end-demand recovery 
impacting capacity utilization; (2) slower customer node migrations; (3) slowdown in AI 
investment resulting in lower long-term semiconductor content growth; (4) poor 
yields/execution resulting in worse-than-expected profitability; (5) stronger 
competition resulting in ASP/profitability erosion; and (6) unfavorable FX trend or 
higher-than-expected cost increase weighing on the margin outlook.

Buy (B), Neutral (N), Sell (S) Analysts recommend stocks as Buys or Sells for inclusion on various regional Investment Lists. Being assigned a Buy or Sell
on an Investment List is determined by a stock’s total return potential relative to its coverage universe. Any stock not assigned as a Buy or a Sell on an
Investment List with an active rating (i.e., a  stock that is not Rating Suspended, Not Rated, Early-Stage Biotech, Coverage Suspended or Not Covered), is

deemed Neutral. Each region manages Regional Conviction Lists, which are selected from Buy rated stocks on the respective region’s Investment Lists
and represent investment recommendations focused on the size of the total return potential and/or the likelihood of the realization of the return across
their respective areas of coverage.  The addition or removal of stocks from such Conviction Lists are managed by the Investment Review Committee or
other designated committee in each respective region and do not represent a change in the analysts’ investment rating for such stocks.

Total return potential represents the upside or downside differential between the current share price and the price target, including all paid or
anticipated dividends, expected during the time horizon associated with the price target. Price targets are required for all covered stocks. The total
return potential, price target and associated time horizon are stated in each report adding or reiterating an Investment List membership.

Company Brief

RA 우서현 
seohyun.woo@meritz.co.kr 3Q26 OP 80.1조원 실적 서프라이즈 예상  
3Q26 영업이익: 상반기와 다르다. 억제 요인들의 추세 반전 전환 
SK하이닉스의 3Q26 영업이익은 전분기 대비 32% 증가한 80.1조원으로 시장기대
치를 능가할 전망이다. 최근 원화강세 흐름 및 상반기 동사의 상대적 실적개선 속도 
열위에 3Q26 영업이익 추정치는 70조원까지 내려갔다 (컨센서스 74조원). 하지만 
이제 동사 출하량 및 판가의 탄력적 복원세가 경쟁사 보다 빠른 실적 개선을 이끌 전
망이다. 3Q26 DRAM과 NAND 출하량/판가 증감률은 각각 +13%/+22%, 
+4%/+15% 수준으로, 각 영업이익은 63조원/17조원으로 예상된다 (OPM 각각 
84%/69% 추정). 그간의 상대적 실적 열위는 이제 반전된다. 이는 1) 판가 전략에 
있어 중장기적 관점에서 고객 중심 유연성 극대화를 꾀했던 부분의 보상적 되돌림이 
발생하고, 2) 업계에서 제일 높은 HBM 판매 비중으로 인해 판가 상승률에 발목 잡혔
지만, 오히려 HBM4 본격 공급이 시작되며 이익기여가 본격화된다. 아울러 3) 모바
일 핵심고객과의 기성 계약이 산업 전반적 LTA 논의로 재검토되며 공급사에 크게 유
리한 조건으로 변화되어 가기 시작한다. 당사는 1Q27부터 모바일 메모리 판가 급등
을 전망하며 (그림1), 2026E/2027E 영업이익의 경우 264조원/454조원으로 상향조정한
다. 4Q26 내 시장 실적 추정치 상향조정 및 투자자 수급의 구조적 개선을 전망한다.

‘지능기술’ 시대, 수요의 가격 역탄력성 속 중장기 기회창출에 주목 
과거 B2C 사이클과 다르게, 금번 B2B 사이클은 수요의 가격 역탄력성을 보이고 있
다. AGI 도달을 위한 선착순 투자가 핵심이기 때문이다. 빅테크 중 낙오자가 발생하
기 전까지 경쟁적 대규모 투자는 지속된다. 최근 동사의 솔리다임 상장 추진보도에 
일부 우려가 제기되고 있지만, 투자자들은 그 이면의 중장기적 기회에 주목해야 한다. 
동사는 LTA 외에도, 다양한 파트너십을 추진하며 중장기 시장 예측력을 증가시키고 
있다. 솔리다임 상장 과정에서 유입되는 현금은 모회사 ‘SK AI co’의 지분교환 및 JV 
구축 등에 활용되리라 예상된다. 이는 중장기 수요를 ‘창출’시키는 개념이다.

순수한 주주가치 제고 목적 환원 재도출 예상. 적정주가 상향조정 
빠르게 증가한 현금흐름 중 ‘잉여’분은 주주가치 ‘회복’에 우선 배분돼야 함이 마땅하
며, 동사 역시 최근 공시를 통해 주주가치 제고와 주가의 저평가 인식을 드러냈다. 현
재 진행중인 40조원 자사주 매입 프로그램 종료 후 즉각적인 신규 환원 정책 도출이 
예상된다. 2027년 메모리 공급부족은 명확하다. 역사적으로 효율적 시장 내, 주가 조
정은 탄력적 복원으로 귀결되어왔다. 동사에 대한 투자의견 Buy를 유지하며, 적정주
가의 경우 320만원으로 상향한다 (26E/27E 평균 P/B 4.0배 적용). 
 Meritz Research  2026. 10. 12

Buy (20거래일 평균종가 대비 상승 여력 기준)

## Document 136: [207940] 삼성바이오로직스(207940) 단기 부담에도 컨센서스 부합 기대삼성바이오로직스(207940) 단기 부담에도 컨센서스 부합 기대단기 부담_2a8cdc97

Source PDF: all report pdf/[207940] 삼성바이오로직스(207940) 단기 부담에도 컨센서스 부합 기대삼성바이오로직스(207940) 단기 부담에도 컨센서스 부합 기대단기 부담_2a8cdc97.pdf

(26.10.02) 
1,354,000 
제약업종

◼ 3Q26 컨센서스 부합, 4Q26 성장폭 확대 전망 
◼ 5공장 Ramp-up 지속, 신규 수주 회복 여부에 주목 
◼ 투자의견 매수, 목표주가 2,000,000원 유지

투자의견 매수, 목표주가 2,000,000원 유지 
목표주가는 DCF 방식으로 산출(WACC 8.1%, 영구성장률 3.5% 적용)

3Q26 Preview: 단기 부담에도 컨센서스 부합 전망 
3Q26 매출액 1조 3,380억원(+6.4% yoy, +1.3% qoq), 영업이익 5,890억원(-
7.0% yoy, +0.4% qoq, OPM 44.0%)으로 컨센서스에 부합하는 실적을 전
망함. 3분기 평균 원/달러 환율이 전분기 대비 약 5% 하락하고, 지난 5월 
파업에 따른 생산 차질 영향이 반영되나, 5공장 PPQ 물량의 매출 인식이 
시작되며 yoy, qoq 외형 성장은 이어질 것으로 예상함.  
파업 당시 생산이 지연된 물량은 3Q 중 추가 생산되어 4Q26부터 매출에 반
영될 전망임. 5공장은 다수 프로젝트의 PPQ가 진행 중이며, Ramp-up에 
따라 매출 기여도 점진적으로 확대될 것으로 예상함. 이에 4Q26에는 파
업 이연 물량 반영과 5공장 매출 확대에 따라 실적 성장폭이 확대될 전
망임. 2026년 연간 매출액과 영업이익은 각각 5조 5,310억원(+21.4% 
yoy), 2조 4,780억원(+19.8% yoy, OPM 44.8%)으로 전망함.

신규 수주 회복 기대, 중장기 성장 기반은 확대 중 
관세 관련 불확실성으로 둔화됐던 고객사와의 수주 논의가 재개되고 있음. 
10월 2일 유럽 소재 제약사와 935억원 규모의 CMO 계약을 추가 체결
했으며, 4Q26 신규 수주 회복 여부에 주목함.  
PolyPeptide 인수는 연내 거래 종결을 목표로 진행 중이며, 인수 완료 
시 펩타이드 분야로 CDMO 포트폴리오가 확대될 전망임. 6 공장도 연내 
착공을 목표로 준비 중임. 
한편, 노사 리스크는 단기 불확실성 요인임. 노조는 10/26부터 3주간 2
차 총파업을 예고함. 1차 대비 예정된 파업 기간이 길어진 만큼 실제 파
업 진행 시 생산 차질 규모가 주요 변수임. 다만 파업 개시 전까지 협상 
가능성이 열려 있어, 향후 노사 협상 진행 경과를 확인할 필요가 있음.

Unit approvals drive the headline lower

Key points
• Total dwelling approvals fell 6.1%mth in
August, with the annual pace easing to
10.3%yr. The headline decline was driven
by a sharp fall in private unit approvals.

• Private detached dwelling approvals
rose 3.7%mth, reaching their highest
level since August 2021. In contrast,
private unit approvals fell 21.2%mth.
• Queensland recorded the weakest
result, while NSW also posted
a sizeable decline. By contrast,
WA, Victoria and South Australia
recorded gains over the month.
• The value of residential building
approvals fell 0.6%mth. Volatility
in the data centre dominated ‘other
commercial buildings’ segment
contributed to the total value of

building approvals falling 21.3%mth.
• While detached dwelling approvals
continue to show resilience, recent
RBA cash rate increases continue
to present risks to the outlook.

### Page 2

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
Total dwelling approvals fell 6.1%mth in August, with the
annual pace easing slightly to 10.3%yr. The result was broadly
in line with Westpac’s expectation for a 5.0%mth decline,
although notably weaker than the market expectation for a
1.0%mth fall. July’s decline in private detached dwellings

proved short lived, with approvals rebounding in August.
Instead, weakness in the headline result was driven by a
sizeable decline in private unit approvals.
Note that most figures in the charts and table are presented on
a rolling-three month basis to smooth out monthly volatility.
Private detached dwelling approvals rose 3.7%mth in August,
following a large 2.5ppt upward revision to July’s 1.9%mth
decline. The increase was the strongest in more than a year,

with private detached dwelling approvals reaching their highest
level since August 2021. With the annual pace strengthening
to 18.4%yr, detached dwelling approvals continue to appear
resilient despite recent interest rate increases. Some of this
resilience may partly reflect the implementation of changes to
SMSF property borrowing arrangements on 10 August.
Private unit approvals drove the decline in the headline result,
falling 21.2%mth in August. Following a 1.2ppt upward

revision to July’s 0.8%mth increase, the annual pace returned to
contractionary territory at –2.1%yr. Weakness was broad-based
across segments, with both high-rise and low-mid-rise
approvals declining in the month.
Queensland recorded the weakest result, with private approvals
falling 22.6%mth, driven primarily by weakness in unit approvals,
while private approvals in NSW also declined, falling 17.1%mth.
By contrast, WA (+0.2%mth), Victoria (+8.4%mth) and South

Australia (+32.0%mth) all recorded gains over the month.
In value terms, residential building approvals fell 0.6%mth,
driven by a 0.5%mth decline in new residential approvals
and a 2.1%mth fall in renovation activity. Non-residential
approvals declined sharply, down 44.8%mth. Volatility
continues to be driven by the data centre-dominated ‘other
commercial’ segment, which fell 91.1%mth on a non-seasonally
adjusted basis. Excluding this segment, in non-seasonally

adjusted terms, non-residential approvals fell a more subdued
13.9%mth (from -37.9%mth). The total value of building
approvals declined 21.3%mth in August.
August’s result suggests dwelling approvals continue to
moderate following the strength recorded earlier in the year.
However, the underlying picture remains more resilient, with
the headline decline driven by volatility in unit approvals
while detached dwellings strengthened. NSW and Victoria,

traditionally among the more interest rate sensitive housing
markets, recorded the weakest detached dwelling outcomes,
potentially signalling broader rate effects ahead. For a
comprehensive assessment of housing market conditions, see
our latest Housing Pulse (see here).
Detached approvals point to some resilience

Top 5 ngành tăng / giảm  VNINDEX giảm điểm trong tuần qua, -2.62 điểm tương ứng -0.15%, với thanh khoản cải 
thiện. Dệt may, Công nghệ và Máy móc là các nhóm ngành có mức giảm rõ rệt nhất trong 
tuần. Khối ngoại duy trì bán ròng so với xu hướng của tuần trước với giá trị -13,987 tỷ 
đồng, tập trung ở các mã HDB, PNJ và VJC; mặt khác, tự doanh đảo chiều bán ròng ở mức 
-30 tỷ đồng.

Ở một khía cạnh khác, khối ngoại đảo chiều mua ròng 1,267 HĐTL VN30F1M trong tuần 
qua nhưng đang duy trì vị thế bán lũy kế -2,363 hợp đồng. Tự doanh mua ròng 3,726 hợp 
đồng nhưng đang duy trì trạng thái lũy kế bán ròng -2,702 hợp đồng.

TTCK Việt Nam trải qua một tuần đi ngang giằng co, giảm nhẹ và đóng cửa ở 1.735 điểm 
với thanh khoản ở mức thấp, và nhà đầu tư nước ngoài bán ròng cả 5 phiên trong tuần. 
Thông tin vĩ mô tích cực với tăng trưởng GDP quý 3 đạt mức gần 10% là điểm sáng trong 
tuần qua, tuy đã không đủ nâng đỡ thị trường chung. Thay vào đó, tâm lý thị trường thận 
trọng trước số liệu lạm phát ở mức cao, đợt tăng giá xăng dầu trong nước và mặt bằng lãi 
suất trong nước chưa có dấu hiệu hạ nhiệt. Diễn biến tăng của giá dầu thế giới, và lợi suất 
trái phiếu Chính phủ Mỹ cũng là các yếu tố gây áp lực lên thị trường chung. Tâm lý thận 
trọng còn thể hiện qua áp lực bán giải chấp ở một số cổ phiếu riêng lẻ như PNJ, SHB..., dù 
một vài nhóm trụ như dầu khí, tiêu dùng có thời điểm đóng vai trò nâng đỡ chỉ số.

Trần Đức Anh 
Giám đốc Kinh tế Vĩ mô & Chiến lược thị trường 
anhtd@kbsec.com.vn 
07/10/2026

Triển vọng TTCK 4Q2026 
Tích lũy trong vùng nhiễu động

Chúng tôi dự báo vùng điểm hợp lý của chỉ số VNIndex thời điểm cuối năm 2026 
xuống 1,800 đi ểm, giảm mạnh từ mức 1,950 đi ểm đưa ra trong báo cáo gần nhất. 
Vùng điểm này tương ứng:

- Tăng trưởng EPS 2026 so với cùng kỳ của thị trường chung ở mức 33% 
(tăng mạnh so với mức 25% của báo cáo gần nhất), chủ yếu nhờ kỳ vọng 
vào các chính sách thúc đẩy kinh tế của Chính phủ, và lợi nhuận tăng 
trưởng đột biến ở VIC (xem thêm phần Triển vọng hoạt động kinh doanh).

- Mức P/E hợp lý của chỉ số VNIndex thời điểm cuối năm ở mức 13.2 (giảm 
nhẹ so với mức 13.5 thời điểm hiện tại - phản ánh quan điểm các yếu tố 
rủi ro sẽ tiếp tục duy trì). Đây là mức định giá thấp so với bình quân 5 năm 
gần nhất ở 15.1. Nếu loại bỏ yếu tố đột biến ở nhóm Vingroup, chỉ số P/E 
điều chỉnh hiện đã rơi xuống 10.9, thấp hơn các đáy gần nhất năm 2020, 
2022 và 2025. Trong bối cảnh kinh tế Việt Nam duy trì tốc độ tăng trưởng 
cao, lợi nhuận các doanh nghiệp niêm yết liên tục bứt phá, vùng định giá 
này mở ra cơ hội đầu tư trung/dài hạn. Chúng tôi cho rằng ngay khi các rủi 
ro vĩ mô quốc tế (xoay quanh vấn đề Iran), và trong nước (xoay quanh vấn 
đề lãi suất) hạ nhiệt, thị trường chung sẽ có cơ hội tái định giá lên mức cao 
hơn đáng kể so với thời điểm hiện tại. Dù vậy, khác với báo cáo gần nhất 
vào tháng 7, ở báo cáo này, chúng tôi đẩy lui thời điểm các yếu tố rủi ro vĩ 
mô hạ nhiệt sang năm 2027, đồng nghĩa với quan điểm thận trọng hơn về 
thị trường cho quý 4 2026.

Cụ thể, đối với vấn đề lãi suất, áp lực lạm phát lớn dần, đi kèm nhu cầu vốn cao phục 
vụ hoạt động đầu tư cơ sở hạ tầng sẽ khiến mặt bằng lãi suất duy trì ở mức cao cho 
phần còn lại của năm 2026 (dù thanh khoản hệ thống ghi nhận sự cải thiện đáng kể 
trong 1 quý gần đây). Đối với vấn đề xung đột Trung Đông, các cuộc đàm phán vẫn 
đang bế tắc và ít có kh ả năng dòng ch ảy dầu mỏ tại khu vực này được nối lại ngay 
trong năm nay.

Chủ đề đầu tư chúng tôi l ựa chọn cho 4Q2026 là đ ầu tư công, chính sách ti ền tệ hỗ 
trợ, và xung đột Iran.

Xem đầy đủ tại đây.

Ngưỡng kỹ thuật

VNINDEX hình thành mẫu nến Doji trên khung đồ thị tuần sau hai tuần giảm điểm liên tiếp, 
cho thấy áp lực bán đã phần nào được kiểm soát. Trên khung ngày, nỗ lực hồi phục đầu 
tuần gặp lực cản tại đường SMA50 khiến VNINDEX quay đầu giảm điểm, tiếp tục chớm phá 
đáy ngắn hạn; tuy nhiên vùng hỗ trợ gần đã tạo hiệu ứng thu hút lực cầu bắt đáy, giúp chỉ 
số thu hẹp đà giảm trong phiên cuối tuần. Sau nhịp điều chỉnh khá tiêu cực từ vùng đỉnh 
đầu tháng 9, VNINDEX đang bước vào giai đoạn tìm kiếm điểm cân bằng trở lại và diễn biến 
giằng co điều chỉnh có thể còn kéo dài thêm trong các phiên kế tiếp. Tuy nhiên khi xu 
hướng tăng ngắn hạn tính từ vùng đáy cuối tháng 7 vẫn đang được bảo lưu, chỉ số được kỳ 
vọng sẽ có cơ hội hồi phục trở lại tại điểm đỡ gần quanh 1710 (đây cũng là chốt chặn cần 
giữ để bảo vệ được xu hướng tăng ngắn hạn). 
Kháng cự xa: 1820 (+-15) 
Kháng cự gần: 1775 (+-10) 
Hỗ trợ gần: 1710 (+-10) 
Hỗ trợ xa: 1670 (+-15)

Danh mục đầu tư mẫu

Phương pháp tiếp cận: 
- Danh mục tập hợp các cổ phiếu tiềm năng nhất được KBSV 
lựa chọn và tái cơ cấu theo tháng. 
- Ngưỡng cắt lỗ cần được cân nhắc cho mỗi mã là -15%. 
- Danh mục giả định luôn duy trì tỷ trọng 100% là cổ phiếu với 
trọng số được phân bổ đều giữa các mã.

Lưu ý:  
- NĐT được khuyến nghị mua tại mức giá bằng hoặc thấp hơn 
giá khuyến nghị mua. 
- Lợi nhuận kỳ vọng được tính theo giá khuyến nghị mua. 
 So sánh hiệu suất với VN30 Index

Danh mục đầu tư mẫu 
Mã Giá đóng cửa tuần Biến động từ đầu tháng Giá khuyến nghị mua Giá mục tiêu Lợi nhuận  kỳ vọng Điểm nhấn đầu tư Báo cáo phân tích

Năm 2026 dự kiến mở thêm 400-450 cửa hàng (+20%YoY) với DT trên mỗi cửa hàng cũ tăng 
trưởng 6%-8%/năm 
- Dư địa lấy thêm thị phần từ các cửa hàng nhỏ lẻ vẫn còn tương đối rộng, FRT đặt mục tiêu 
chiếm 50% thị phần doanh thu ngành bán lẻ dược phẩm, từ mức 17%-20% hiện tại 
- Tiềm năng dài hạn từ mảng tiêm chủng tương đối tích cực

### Page 2

2 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Stronger Growth, Costlier Money
Chief Investment Office, Macro Strategy Team
The latest GDP report and annual revisions show a sturdier U.S. economy than previously
reported. According to the Bureau of Economic Analysis (BEA), real GDP grew at a 2.2%
annualized pace in Q2 compared to 1.5% previously estimated, and Q1 was revised
from 2.1% to 2.5%. Both 2024 and 2025 were also revised slightly higher. Most of the

first half of the year revision was in categories related to the Artificial Intelligence (AI)
buildout, but real consumer spending has also been revised slightly higher, accelerating
from 0.7% annualized growth in Q1 to 3.8% in Q2 despite surging energy prices and
slow hiring.
Spending in July and August remained much stronger than labor market conditions alone
would suggest, also averaging 3.7% annualized growth. Upside revisions to net interest

income and personal saving suggest households have benefited from more income than
was previously apparent, with personal saving revised higher back to 2023. The new data,
according to the BEA, show the January to July 2026 savings rate averaged 4.8% versus
3.3% prerevision, strengthening consumer spending underpinnings despite moderate
hiring, high energy prices and rising borrowing costs.
The BEA update also included inflation methodological changes related to categories

inflated by the AI boom—such as portfolio management fees and computer software and
accessories. These improved the inflation picture to some extent—August “core” PCE
inflation was reduced by 0.3 ppts to 3% YoY, bringing it closer to “core” consumer price
index inflation, which was 2.5% YoY and 2% on a three-month annualized basis. While still
above the 2% Federal Reserve (Fed) target, “core” PCE now appears stable at 3% year over
year since January rather than accelerating. These methodological changes are expected

to dampen pass-through of AI-related price pressures into “core” inflation and effects of
stock-market swings on inflation.
They also produced an encouraging improvement in recent PCE inflation momentum.
Three-month annualized “core” PCE inflation slowed from 4.3% at the peak of tariff
effects in February to 2.1% in August. For durable goods, this rate was down from 5.8%
in February to 1.5% in August, and, for services ex-energy and healthcare, it slowed from

4.2% to 2.2%, for example. Inflation is volatile on a three-month change basis, so it
remains to be seen whether this moderation continues without further Fed tightening.
For now, the trends in the San Francisco Fed’s cyclical versus acyclical inflation measures
diverged again in August. As expected, given softening wage growth, prices most sensitive
to the labor market have eased further. At 3% YoY, this inflation category is down from
8% in early 2023 and 4.6% two years ago is back to prepandemic levels and is likely

to ease more (Exhibit 1A). In contrast, supply bottlenecks, high energy prices and other
industry-specific factors have kept acyclical inflation on an uptrend. As shown in Exhibit
1B, the relentless increase in freight costs could prolong upside pressure on this category.
The diesel market has tightened in recent months, boosting its price by 70% YoY,
consistent with Brent oil at around $200 per barrel and large reverberations across
a wide swath of goods and services prices. A government-policy-related decline in

available truck drivers is also raising transportation costs more than expected. So are
tight ocean shipping capacity and longer/more expensive voyages due to the U.S./Iran
War. Supply-chain stress also increased as companies rushed to front-run potential new
tariff increases.
On the positive side for the inflation outlook, unit labor costs (ULC) are up modestly
as robust productivity growth has absorbed much of the wage growth, limiting a major
source of domestic inflation. Upside productivity growth revisions are likely to further

reduce ULC, which could help shift focus from energy and AI-related inflation to
eventual disinflationary effects once oil and refined fuel prices start to ease. Sustained
productivity gains would support growth and margins while limiting inflation and the
need for much higher interest rates.
Investment Implications
We favor selective Equity exposure
to companies with durable demand
and pricing power, while remaining
cautious about businesses most

exposed to fuel, freight, and
financing costs. We would also
gradually increase long-duration
exposure.

### Page 3

3 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Surging nominal growth has boosted corporate revenues (up 13% YoY in Q2, according to the
BEA) while contained unit labor costs helped raise margins and profits more than expected.
With labor costs declining as a share of revenues, investment-related tax incentives and
rock-bottom net interest expense, pre-tax nonfinancial corporate profits as measured in GDP
accounts rose 21% YoY in Q2. Total corporate profits after-tax were up 18.2% YoY, according to

the BEA.
Surging AI-related imports and investment are keeping the business and profits cycle on
an uptrend both here and abroad. According to BofA Global Research, the global economic
cycle backdrop improved further in September, as reflected in a broad-based increase in its
Global Wave1 indicator. Broadening U.S. manufacturing strength, fueled by ongoing AI-driven
investment, a healthier orders backlog and low inventories suggest that the expansion has

room to run—a constructive environment for earnings. Typically, a Global Wave upswing is
consistent with rising earnings revisions ratios and equity price uptrends. According to the
Global Wave, global earnings growth expectations remain exceptionally strong.
Still, absent a resolution of war-related energy and transportation disruptions, the same
industrial strength intensifies inflation pressures. A prolonged supply shock raises the risk of
restrictive interest rates and weakening economic growth. In this context, the surge in the

10-year Treasury yield above 5% is starting to reflect more than optimism about U.S. growth
and strengthening productivity outlook. Oil’s tightest recent correlation with 10-year Treasury
yields in 35 years suggests a common energy-price thread behind rising yields in developed
countries.
Bond market spillovers from normalizing Japan yields and capital repatriation are another
common thread. Higher yields on foreign sovereign bonds such as in Japan—which has

escaped stagnation and deflation by intensely pursuing investment-led growth—weaken the
relative appeal of Treasurys. That distinction matters for portfolios. A yield increase driven
mainly by durable productivity gains would come with a stronger outlook for future income
and earnings. A yield increase driven by cost shocks and greater uncertainty raises financing
costs without the same benefit.
In sum, the economy remains more resilient than expected, but the bond market is also

charging more for an uncertain inflation outlook given untethered government debt and
cooling global demand for Treasurys spurred by geopolitical tensions and Japan capital
repatriation. The case for much further tightening becomes stronger if wage growth
strengthens, productivity lags, and unit labor costs reaccelerate, causing cyclical inflation to
also pick up steam. The case becomes weaker if that measure stays contained while fuel and
freight pressures recede or rising Treasury yields reduce asset prices and economic growth.

4 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Yields, Yen and Iran: What Haunts the “House”
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
We recently highlighted the three-front challenge facing Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent:
engineering a stronger yen, containing the 10-year Treasury yield and securing a diplomatic
resolution to the conflict with Iran.

Bessent recently has delivered one of the year’s more memorable lines while discussing
Treasury policy and the yen: “I have asymmetric information. I am the house now.” 2 Yet several
challenges remain: the 10-year Treasury yield is near levels last seen in the early 2000s, the
yen remains structurally weaker, and a phased agreement to reopen the Strait of Hormuz
appears remote. The resulting strain on energy markets keeps inflation risks elevated.

Put differently, oil prices, Treasury yields and the dollar-yen exchange rate are all trending
against Bessent’s objectives. Below, we examine how each variable is haunting his “house.”
Haunting the “House” is the Yen. Since the U.S. and Japan mounted a record $98.7B
coordinated intervention in late July after the yen weakened to a 40-year low near 164 per
dollar, the currency has appreciated roughly 4% against the dollar. The move was further
supported on September 18 when the BoJ raised rates by 25 basis points (bps) to 1.25%,

their highest level since the mid-1990s (Exhibit 2A). However, the split 7–2 vote and Governor
Ueda’s limited forward guidance leave the path of further tightening uncertain. The key
question is whether additional BoJ hikes can produce sustained appreciation in the yen, or
merely reinforce the temporary relief provided by the summer intervention.
Bessent, meanwhile, has repeatedly signaled his preference for a stronger yen. Partly because
yen stability is also a U.S. financing concern. Persistent currency weakness could prompt

Japan to support its currency by selling foreign assets, including U.S. Treasurys—which would
be an unwelcome prospect as Washington partially relies on foreign capital flows to fund its
large fiscal deficits. That potential “haunts” the Treasury Secretary.  At precisely the moment
Washington needs foreign capital to finance its enormous fiscal deficits, it would be inopportune
for one of America’s largest creditors to become a significant seller of Treasury securities.

Haunting the “House” are Yields. The surge in yields runs counter to a White House intent
on lowering borrowing costs. The benchmark 10-year yield has broken above the 5% threshold
and climbed to levels not seen since 2007. At around 5.30%, it stands more than a full
percentage point above where it began the year (Exhibit 2B). The level is striking but so is the
speed of the move: Stronger-than-expected economic data, including a survey showing U.S.

2 September 8, 2026.
Investment Implications
While some key variables are
complicating the macro backdrop
(i.e., higher-for-longer yields/oil)
the combination of strong earnings
growth, robust consumer demand
(real final sales to private domestic
purchasers grew at 4.6% for Q2) and
ongoing capital investment suggests
that the fundamental backdrop for
U.S. Equities remains constructive.

5 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
business activity expanded at the fastest pace in more than five years, helped trigger an 18
bps surge in the 10-year yield—among its largest daily moves in decades. The 5-year yield also
topped 5% for the first time since 2007, while the 30-year yield reached 5.63%, its highest
level since 2002.
Compounding the move is inflation, running above the Fed’s 2% target for 67 months,

large fiscal deficits and the prospect of continued growth in Treasury issuance. Against this
backdrop, Treasury bought back $4.1 billion of long-dated debt, versus a maximum of $6
billion. That effort has done little to arrest the selloff. A subsequent auction priced nearly $4
billion of 7-year notes at 5.085%, the highest yield for that maturity in 33 years.
All in, the MOVE index, a key gauge of Treasury market volatility, jumped from 78 to 95 on

Wednesday September 23 and then to 104 the following day, its highest level since the early
days of the Iran war (Exhibit 3A).
Haunting the “House” is the Iran conflict. Early expectations that military pressure and
economic sanctions would quickly bring Iran to the negotiating table have yet to materialize.
Iranian officials have signaled that a full reopening of the Strait of Hormuz remains unlikely
while sanctions stay in place. Although talks between Washington and Tehran are continuing,

conflicting signals and lingering skepticism from both sides have kept tensions elevated.
As a result, oil prices have remained elevated in a range even as some Middle East supply
disruptions began to ease.
Startling is that Brent crude is up more than 60% this year, but refined product prices
have risen even faster. Ongoing disruptions to energy flows through the Strait of Hormuz,
combined with Ukrainian strikes on Russian refining infrastructure have tightened global

supplies of crude oil and refined fuels. Saudi Arabia has restored some drone-disrupted flows,
providing roughly 3.5 million barrels a day of relief (roughly 3% of global consumption).
The squeeze is mostly acute in diesel markets, where inventories remain constrained and
prices continue to climb (Exhibit 3B). U.S. diesel prices recently surpassed $6.50 per gallon,
the highest level on record, just as Russia has extended restrictions on diesel exports into

October. Against this backdrop, proposals to ban U.S. fuel exports risks worsening the
imbalance. According to S&P Global, an export ban could reduce refinery runs by nearly 2
million barrels per day and significantly cut gasoline production, potentially driving gasoline
and jet fuel prices even higher. As long as tensions persist, the oil impulse remains a key
impediment to structurally lower inflation.
Investment implications. Treasury Secretary Bessent increasingly sits at the intersection of

monetary policy, fiscal policy, foreign policy and national security. We remain constructive on
the U.S. economy and overweight U.S. Equities but are watching as these fronts challenge the
narrative. There is a great deal riding on the “house” versus the key macro variables of today.

6 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
When It Comes to Attracting Foreign Capital, the U.S. Remains Exceptional
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
There may be no better vote of confidence in a country than the willingness of
foreigners to put their money there. And by that measurement, the U.S. remains the
exception—both portfolio and FDI inflows are currently running at record highs based

on the latest data. This dynamic is one key reason why we remain constructive on U.S.
assets over the longer term.
Notwithstanding all the handwringing about America’s fiscal deficits, tariffs and rising
government debt, global investors sunk some $942B in net purchases of U.S. Equities in
the 12 months to July, the highest rolling 12-month total on record. In Q2, net purchase
of U.S. Equities soared to $426B, up 62% from the same period a year ago (Exhibit 4A).

By country, flows were down from China—not surprisingly—but strong from Taiwan and
South Korea, who have taken to recycling their massive AI-generated trade surplus back
into the U.S. In the end, strong portfolio inflows suggest that international investors
remain willing to increase their exposure to the world's largest capital market—that’s
bullish for U.S. assets.
Meanwhile, Exhibit 4B highlights the growing push among foreign multinationals to be

inside the U.S., with FDI inflows totaling nearly $240B in the first six months of this year.
That is a staggering 103% jump from a year ago and means FDI inflows are on track for
a record year, estimated at $480B for 2026.
There are multiple factors behind surging inflows: One, due to rising U.S. trade
protectionism, foreign multinationals increasingly want to be “inside” the U.S. economy,
not outside. Two, within the $32 trillion-plus economy with extraordinarily consumer

purchasing power, foreign firms prefer to be closer to their customers via investment
than just trade (exports). Three, America’s massive investment cycle in AI, data centers,
semiconductors, power generation, etc., has created numerous potential investment
opportunities for many foreign firms that don’t want to miss out on America’s surging
capex cycle. Four, and finally, abundant energy supplies, deep capital markets and a highly
developed technological ecosystem have also induced more FDI inflows.

In the end, double-barreled capital inflows speak to the absolute and relative
attractiveness of the U.S. economy and capital markets. Stay invested in America—the
rest of the world is.
Exhibit 4: U.S. Exceptionalism: Record Inflows of Portfolio and FDI Inflows.
A) Net Foreign Purchases of US Equities and Investment Funds. B) FDI Inflows to the U.S.

Investment Implications
Foreign investors remain keen on
owning and being “inside” the U.S. That
is bullish for U.S. securities, both near-

term and long-term.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

ICE BofA MOVE Index is a market-implied measure of U.S. bond market volatility.
Consumer Price Index measures the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a market basket of consumer goods and services.
Baltic Dry Index is a daily shipping and trade index published by the Baltic Exchange in London that measures the cost of moving raw materials like iron ore, coal, and grain by sea.
Volatility Index (VIX) is a real-time market indicator that measures the stock market's expected volatility over the next 30 days.

Bank of America, Merrill, their affiliates and advisors do not provide legal, tax or accounting advice. Clients should consult their legal and/or tax advisors before making any financial decisions.
This material does not take into account a client’s particular investment objectives, financial situations, or needs and is not intended as a recommendation, offer, or solicitation for the purchase
or sale of any security or investment strategy. Merrill offers a broad range of brokerage, investment advisory and other services. There are important differences between brokerage and

of America, Merrill or any affiliate to sell or provide, or a specific invitation for a consumer to apply for, any particular retail financial product or service that may be available.
The Chief Investment Office (“CIO”) provides thought leadership on wealth management, investment strategy and global markets; portfolio management solutions; due diligence; and solutions
oversight and data analytics. CIO viewpoints are developed for Bank of America Private Bank, a division of Bank of America, N.A., (“Bank of America”) and Merrill Lynch, Pierce, Fenner & Smith

The Global Wealth & Investment Management Investment Strategy Committee (“GWIM ISC”) is responsible for developing and coordinating recommendations for short-term and long-term
investment strategy and market views encompassing markets, economic indicators, asset classes and other market-related projections affecting GWIM.
BofA Global Research is research produced by BofA Securities, Inc. (“BofAS”) and/or one or more of its affiliates. BofAS is a registered broker-dealer, Member SIPC and wholly owned subsidiary

of Bank of America Corporation.
All recommendations must be considered in the context of an individual investor’s goals, time horizon, liquidity needs and risk tolerance. Not all recommendations will be in the best interest
of all investors.
Asset allocation, diversification and rebalancing do not ensure a profit or protect against loss in declining markets.
Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.
Nonfinancial assets, such as closely held businesses, real estate, fine art, oil, gas and mineral properties, and timber, farm and ranch land, are complex in nature and involve risks including total

Miha Hribernik | Mapping the Next Phase of China-US Ties |  September 2026
The Trump-Xi meeting in Washington met our key expectation: It delivered a subdued-yet-constructive summit resulting in a modest number of concrete deliverables that will minimise the near-term risks of abrupt
decoupling, a security crisis, or a disruptive trade war. This was the best plausible outcome for markets, given the structural nature of China-US competition. The brief two-month extension of the Busan agreement until

10 Jan 2027 will further delay new tariffs and export controls on both sides. It will also increase pressure to deliver a broader trade and investment package, and further trade-truce extensions, by the end of the year. We
anticipate more talks between US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent and Chinese Vice Premier He Lifeng in Q4 as both sides seek a larger deal.
For China, stability with Washington will likely remain a priority as it manages rising trade tensions with the EU and prepares for top leadership reshuffles at its 2027 Party Congress. Combined with shifting US public

perceptions of China, these factors could create near-term space for more dealmaking on the sidelines of the upcoming APEC (18-19 Nov) and G20 (14-15 Dec) summits. As our surveys below show, US voters are now
more open to increased trade and investment with China, even at the cost of geopolitical concessions, while concerns over the cost of living have intensified. Both trends are now at their highest levels since April.
Nevertheless, political guardrails are not a panacea for a relationship that remains characterized by mistrust and competing national security priorities. As the next slide will show, the outlook for China-US relations

becomes more complex beyond this near-term horizon….

An analysis of the geopolitical calendar reveals a complex slate of tariffs, import curbs, export controls, and sensitive pol itical events. Even as leader-level diplomacy has reduced the risk of a near-term breakdown, it
has done little to stem the tide of new bilateral economic restrictions. These have proliferated during 2026 to include new e xport controls, sanctions, and tariffs (see list), with significant implications for high-growth

industries including drones, AI, electric vehicles, and robotics.
Competition is unlikely to slow, and events could repeatedly test the fragile trade truce. Q4 2026 will see further China-US dialogue and China-EU trade talks. Should talks with Brussels fall short of averting increased
European trade pressure, Beijing may further prioritise near-term US stability. But the coming months will also bring challenges in the form of new US overcapacity tariffs and polysilicon levies, while next year will usher

in curbs on critical minerals and additional semiconductor tariffs. The run-up to the 10 Jan Busan truce expiry could also bring new economic restrictions as both sides seek negotiating leverage. Both countries will also
hold major political events (China’s Party Congress in 2027 and US elections in 2028), with potential implications for bilateral ties, while Taiwan’s Jan 2028 election will be another key watchpoint.
But these are just the events whose outcomes and impacts we can estimate in advance. Understanding probable China-US developments that are not on the calendar is equally important for investors. We should

assume that competitive dynamics across myriad policy issues will drive other developments and policy changes, often announce d with little or no advance notice. We examine some of the key issues in the next slide.

### Page 4

Miha Hribernik | Mapping the Next Phase of China-US Ties |  September 2026
Unless consistently managed, structural competition could move markets and test stability through the end of the
decade and beyond. We highlight five watchpoints for markets:
1. The AI race: Dialogue is set to strengthen AI safety guardrails, but national and economic security imperatives
mean it is unlikely to significantly slow the China-US AI race, much less meaningfully reverse existing restrictions.

Any attempts to pace AI development would touch on questions of tech restrictions, including Beijing’s perceptions
of US technological containment, which could increase amid potential restrictions on remote access to AI chips and
Chinese data centre components.
2. Tariff pressures: Trade tensions have subsided given a lower US trade deficit and the new Board of Trade, which is
set to reduce tariffs on US$60 billion of non-sensitive goods. But significant differences persist: Washington plans

to unveil new polysilicon and semiconductor tariffs, as well as overcapacity levies on 16 economies (including
China). The US is also seeking to restrict Chinese inputs in North American supply chains and target alleged
transshipment, while Beijing shores up its supply chain dominance in areas of US import dependence, such as
advanced drone components and processed critical minerals – which continue to be a source of friction.
3. Regulatory toolkits: The wider tech sector remains central to expanding export controls and restrictions. China is

strengthening its toolkit. Its 2026 codification of anti-derisking regulations strengthens Beijing’s ability to take
action over foreign sanctions or export controls. Meanwhile, its outbound investment regulations, effective 1 Jul,
cover technology, data, talent and capital. In the US, Congress is drafting legislation to change outbound
investment reporting requirements for biotech and biopharma, restrict government procurement of humanoid
robots and solar equipment, and ban the use of China-linked connected vehicles on military installations.

4. Geopolitical stress-tests: The war in Iran is unlikely to derail relations in the near term, but the conflict has strained
bilateral ties. Any potential broadening of the conflict in scale and intensity, or a further expansion of US sanctions
that impact Chinese interests, could test guardrails. Additionally, while the probability of conflict in the Taiwan
Strait remains low, differences over Taiwan could remain unresolved across successive administrations in both

Washington and Beijing into the 2030s and beyond.
5. Political anchors: Two domestic events will shape relations into the 2030s. Beijing’s priority is stability ahead of the
late 2027 Party Congress. For China, dialogue can help reduce the risk of costly escalation, potentially slow the
pace of new US economic restrictions, and buy time for efforts to strengthen self-reliance in strategic sectors such
as semiconductors (where China is aiming for 80% self-sufficiency by 2030).

In the US, a durable bipartisan consensus on China could make visible concessions to Beijing increasingly difficult
ahead of the 2028 presidential election. Recent bipartisan criticism of the Trump administration’s decision to allow
limited Nvidia H200 chip sales to China reflects the political constraints facing a transactional approach to China,
raising the prospect that guardrails will strain closer to election day.

## Document 308: The Bond Yield Spectrum

Source PDF: all report pdf/The Bond Yield Spectrum.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-10-08
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: The Bond Yield Spectrum
Date:    5 hours ago
The Bond Yield Spectrum

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
The Bond Yield Spectrum
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 7 Oct 2026
View in browser
Photo by Adam Kring / Unsplash

I. From The Good To The Bad To The Ugly
The spectrum of explanations for the rise in bond yields worldwide
includes relatively benign ones to downright dangerous ones (chart). On
the good side of the spectrum, better-than-expected economic growth has
driven US bond yields up to levels that are back to normal. On the ugly
side, soaring bond yields in France are signaling a looming debt crisis. In
between are bad scenarios that explain the synchronized rise in global

### Page 2

bond yields. These include the inflationary energy shock attributable to the
war in the Middle East and the unwinding of the yen-carry trade.
Let's have a closer look at the good, the bad, and the ugly:
(1) R-star rising. R-star, or the neutral interest rate, is the real interest
rate consistent with low unemployment and price stability. It balances the
supply of savings with demand for investment. Three forces are pushing it
higher.
First, demand for capital is booming along with AI-related capital

spending. Hyperscaler capex is expected to reach $750-$800 billion this
year and $1.2 trillion next. Total US corporate bond issuance over the past
12 months through August was a record $3.0 trillion, including $1.4 trillion
and $1.6 trillion issued by nonfinancial and financial corporations,
respectively (chart).

### Page 3

At the same time, US Treasury borrowing totaled $2.1 trillion over the past
12 months through September, including $1.3 trillion in notes and bonds
(chart).
Second, national savings faces demographic headwinds as retiring Baby
Boomers stop saving and draw down their net worth (chart).

### Page 4

Third, stronger productivity growth may be raising the return on capital
and the economy’s sustainable growth rate (chart).
The Fed's 175bps cuts in the federal funds rate since September 2024
have been completely offset by a comparable increase in the 10-year
Treasury bond yield since then. Even at the start of this year, most Fed
officials believed that the FFR was modestly restrictive, i.e., that it
exceeded the neutral rate. In September, they acknowledged their error by

hiking the FFR by 25 basis points, as Fed Chair Kevin Warsh said they
had removed a "dose of accommodation," implying that the FFR is
actually below the neutral rate!

### Page 5

(2) Energy shock. Moving toward the middle of the spectrum are higher-
for-longer oil prices. With US-Iran talks stalled and the US increasing its
military assets, the risk of renewed escalation remains high. The longer
energy costs stay elevated, the greater the risk they'll boost core inflation,
forcing a more aggressive Fed response. If crude oil prices fall because
more oil is getting through the Strait of Hormuz, diesel prices might remain
elevated (chart).

(3) Unwinding yen-carry trade. The yen-carry trade was based on ultra-
low Japanese interest rates and a stable or weak yen. Now the BOJ is
raising interest rates and the yen may be bottoming (charts). This is
forcing traders to liquidate bond holdings previously financed with cheap
Japanese money.

### Page 6

(4) Debt crisis. At the far end of the spectrum is the "Revenge of the
Bond Vigilantes." Between the Great Financial Crisis and the Great Virus
Crisis, major central banks' ultra-easy policies enabled governments to
run large deficits. The Bond Vigilantes were powerless. But now they are
more powerful than ever because government debt is at record highs.
Central banks have been forced to raise their policy rates as inflation has
been more troublesome in recent years. Bond yields have soared this

year worldwide as inflation rebounded and fiscal-risk premiums have
risen.
The danger is a self-reinforcing debt spiral: Higher yields raise interest
costs, widen deficits, require more borrowing, and push yields higher still
(chart). And, of course, higher yields increase the risk of causing a
recession, which would exacerbate any debt crisis.

### Page 7

In our view, the US should remain in the higher R-star end of the
spectrum. We would start to worry about the debt-crisis danger zone if the
US bond yield rises above nominal GDP growth (chart).
In France, on the other hand, the Bond Vigilantes are pushing the 10-year
government bond yield well above nominal GDP growth (chart). The
French could be toast if yields continue to soar.
II. The Fed Minutes
The latest Fed minutes paint a familiar picture: a resilient economy, a firm

labor market, and inflation that remains too high. We drew three key
takeaways from the minutes of the Fed’s September 15-16 meeting.

### Page 8

(1) Restrictiveness. Several participants said the current policy rate was
not restrictive or only mildly restrictive, while a couple explicitly raised their
estimates of the neutral federal funds rate. That fits neatly with our R-star
story.
(2) Macro backdrop. The Fed saw a resilient economy: real GDP is
expanding at a solid pace, consumer spending has firmed, AI investment
remains robust, and the labor market is close to maximum employment. At

the same time, inflation remains elevated and risks were still skewed to
the upside.
(3) Rate outlook. All participants backed September’s 25bps hike, and
most judged that another increase would likely be appropriate by year-
end.
💡
Join the discussion with Ed below! To leave comments or questions,
log in to the Yardeni QuickTakes website and post them at the end
of the QuickTakes article. Paid members' contributions may be
featured in our segment, "Ed Answers Your Questions".

clause.

## Document 325: Top of Mind - Assessing a Less Transparent Fed

Source PDF: all report pdf/Top of Mind - Assessing a Less Transparent Fed.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs (Top of Mind)
Date: 2026-08-25
Pages: 26
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The Goldman Sachs Group, Inc.
Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh is steering the Fed into a less transparent era. So, is that
a positive or negative development, and what could it mean for markets and the
economy? We speak with Former Fed governors Donald Kohn and Stephen Miran
as well as GS’ Jan Hatzius who agree that rigid “Odyssean” forward guidance that
commits policymakers to a pre-set path risks policy mistakes and is therefore rarely
appropriate. But they somewhat disagree about the benefits of great er transparency

around the Fed’s reaction function. Hatzius and Kohn argue that such transparency
helps markets better anticipate and price in policy changes, accelerating monetary
policy transmission. But Miran believes that the line between committing to a path
and being transparent is clearer in theory than in practice. And while GS economists
and strategists find that reduc ed transparency leads to increased market volatility,
the question is whether this just increases the market’s noise or also improves the

market’s signal. Hatzius argues largely for the former and Miran for the latter.
Jan Hatzius, Chief Economist and Head of Global Investment Research, Goldman Sachs
“Markets price what they think the Fed will do, not what they think the Fed should do. That will remain true even if the Fed
obscures its reaction function… market guesses about the Fed’s next moves will simply become worse, leading to more
volatility... and importantly, volatility that serves no constructive economic purpose.”

Donald Kohn, former Vice Chairman of the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, Robert V . Roosa
Chair in International Economics and Senior Fellow, Brookings Institution
“I sympathize with Chairman Warsh to some extent. [But] the real problem with Warsh’s approach is his reluctance to articulate
how the FOMC sees the economy evolving… explaining the Fed’s thinking about the economic outlook is essential in helping
markets act as a stabilizing force and is a core component of accountability.”

We provide a brief snapshot on the most important economies for the global markets

### Page 3

El
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 3 
Top of Mind Issue 151

New Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh is steering the Fed into a less 
transparent era characterized by shorter post-meeting 
statements, less forward guidance, and a more limited role for 
projections, marking a meaningful break from the 
transparency revolution that has defined central banking over 
the last quarter century. So, what could a less transparent Fed 
mean for markets and the economy? 
For answers, we turn to former Fed governors Donald Kohn, 
now Robert V. Roosa Chair in International Economics and 
senior fellow at the Brookings Institution, and Stephen Miran, 
now senior strategist at Hudson Bay Capital Management, as 
well as Jan Hatzius, GS’ Chief Economist and Head of Global 
Investment Research. 
We first ask whether scaling back Fed communication is a 
positive or negative development. Hatzius says it’s likely 
negative, though he draws a clear line between transparency 
and forward guidance. He argues that clarity about the Fed’s 
reaction function helps markets better anticipate policy 
changes and quickly incorporate new information into financial 
conditions, thereby accelerating monetary policy transmission 
to the economy. So, he believes that unwinding that 
transparency would be a mistake. (Note: GS Global 
Economists Joseph Briggs and Megan Peters lay out the 
evidence backing this argument on pgs. 14-15.) 
But Hatzius takes a more nuanced view of forward guidance, 
arguing that rigid “Odyssean” guidance that commits 
policymakers to a pre-set path regardless of new information 
only makes sense at the effective lower bound for the policy 
rate, while conditional “Delphic” guidance tied to the 
economic outlook is generally more defensible. Although 
markets could confuse the two, he believes that most 
sophisticated investors understand the difference and is 
therefore not particularly worried that markets have become 
overly reliant on the policy dots published in the Fed’s 
Summary of Economic Projections (SEP). Instead, he 
considers them a “useful source of additional information 
about the Fed’s reaction function” (except for the long-run 
policy dot). If anything, Hatzius would prefer the dots move 
in an even more informative direction—by publishing each 
policymaker’s combined projections for growth, inflation, 
unemployment, and the policy rate on an anonymized basis. 
Kohn agrees that clarity about the Fed’s “narrative” and 
reaction function is vital. Explaining how the Fed sees the 
economy evolving and how policy would respond, he says, is 
“essential in helping markets act as a stabilizing force and is 
a core component of accountability.” Still, he sympathizes 
somewhat with Chairman Warsh’s view that the dot plot is 
“not helpful” (see pg. 9 for Warsh’s own words on this), 
arguing that the market’s hyper-focus on the SEP’s median 
policy dot “illustrates the risk of excessive precision and 
false certainty,” as small shifts in it can trigger unwarranted 
market moves. So, he has long favored omitting the median. 
Miran, though, is more supportive of Warsh’s push for a less 
transparent Fed. Like Hatzius and Kohn, he’s concerned that 
excessive forward guidance that locks the Fed into a policy 
path raises the risk of policy errors and also argues that such 
guidance can impair market pricing of risk. But he’s less 
convinced about the benefits of increased transparency 
around the Fed’s reaction function, making the case that the 
distinction between the Fed committing to a pre-set policy 
path and being transparent about its reaction function is clear 
in theory, but less so in practice. So, he thinks “the policy dots 
absolutely have to go” but feels less strongly about omitting 
the SEP’s economic projections. 
But would less transparency allow markets to provide the Fed 
with more “direct and unfiltered” information about the 
economy, as Warsh has argued? The answer seems to boil 
down to the implications for market volatility, and whether any 
resulting volatility would simply increase the market’s noise or 
also improve its signal. 
As for the volatility implications, the evidence is fairly clear. 
Briggs and Peters find that increased central bank 
transparency has led to a reduction in market volatility over the 
last several decades. This is consistent with GS Head of US 
Rates Strategy William Marshall’s recent observation of the 
reverse: that the US rates market has already priced 
substantial uncertainty associated with a less transparent Fed, 
with the July FOMC representing the largest non-cut meeting 
day surprise in almost three decades.  
But Marshall argues that whether such meeting-day surprises 
lead to structurally higher rates volatility depends (again) on 
whether the Fed’s reaction function is well understood. If it’s 
not, he would expect higher rates volatility to persist. GS FX 
Strategists Michael Cahill and Lexi Kanter find a similar 
pattern in FX: that central bank communication shifts can 
change which events and data drive FX volatility without 
necessarily raising it, but less transparency could increase 
Dollar volatility—all else equal (though global macro 
conditions remain its biggest driver).  
But the key debate seems to be whether the increased 
volatility resulting from less Fed transparency will improve or 
worsen the market’s signal. Hatzius is clearly in the “worsen” 
camp. He argues that markets price what they think the Fed 
will do—not what it should do—and less transparency won’t 
change that; it would simply produce worse guesses. So, he 
says that while volatility in and of itself is not necessarily bad, 
volatility driven by uncertainty about the Fed’s reaction 
function would serve no constructive economic purpose. 
Kohn agrees that the lack of a clear Fed narrative could create 
damaging uncertainty. But he argues that removing overly 
specific guidance could help free markets to process new 
information more independently and isn’t too worried about 
modestly higher volatility as a result. And Miran seems most 
convinced that reducing transparency would improve the 
markets’ signal, with volatility an unavoidable but acceptable 
cost: “The first best world of signal without volatility doesn't 
exist. I'd rather have that signal, even if it's messy, than 
throw the signal out by anchoring market expectations to the 
Fed's near-term projections.”
